## Earlier published position reading copy: McIntyre, Executor of Estate of McIntyre, Deceased v. Ohio Elections Commission, No. 93-986

Source: [OT_1994CHUNK4.md](../../OT1994/output/OT_1994CHUNK4.md). Copied verbatim; its published scope controls.

### McIntyre, Executor of Estate of McIntyre, Deceased v. Ohio Elections Commission, No. 93-986

Merits decision, April 19, 1995

#### Chronology and Posture

Argued October 12, 1994; decided April 19, 1995. On writ of certiorari to the Supreme Court of Ohio, reviewing its September 22, 1993 judgment, 67 Ohio St.3d 391, sustaining an identification requirement as applied to Margaret McIntyre's leaflets opposing a school-tax levy.

Margaret distributed the leaflets on April 27 and 28, 1988; some identified her and some did not. Family assistance and placement on windshields do not establish an organization or campaign committee. The Commission imposed a $100 fine on March 30, 1990, after the March 30, 1989 complaint. The trial court held the statute unconstitutional as applied; the court of appeals reinstated the fine, and the Ohio Supreme Court affirmed. Margaret died during the litigation, and the executor of her estate continued this challenge. No fraud or falsity in her message was adjudicated. The questions are the scrutiny governing identification on this individual's political leaflets and whether Ohio's information and fraud-prevention interests justify this application.

Ohio Rev. Code Ann. §3599.09(A) (1988) provides:

> No person shall write, print, post, or distribute, or cause to be written, printed, posted, or distributed, a notice, placard, dodger, advertisement, sample ballot, or any other form of general publication which is designed to promote the nomination or election or defeat of a candidate, or to promote the adoption or defeat of any issue, or to influence the voters in any election, or make an expenditure for the purpose of financing political communications through newspapers, magazines, outdoor advertising facilities, direct mailings, or other similar types of general public political advertising, or through flyers, handbills, or other nonperiodical printed matter, unless there appears on such form of publication in a conspicuous place or is contained within said statement the name and residence or business address of the chairman, treasurer, or secretary of the organization issuing the same, or the person who issues, makes, or is responsible therefor. The disclaimer 'paid political advertisement' is not sufficient to meet the requirements of this division. When such publication is issued by the regularly constituted central or executive committee of a political party, organized as provided in Chapter 3517. of the Revised Code, it shall be sufficiently identified if it bears the name of the committee and its chairman or treasurer. No person, firm, or corporation shall print or reproduce any notice, placard, dodger, advertisement, sample ballot, or any other form of publication in violation of this section. This section does not apply to the transmittal of personal correspondence that is not reproduced by machine for general distribution.
>
> The secretary of state may, by rule, exempt, from the requirements of this division, printed matter and certain other kinds of printed communications such as campaign buttons, balloons, pencils, or like items, the size or nature of which makes it unreasonable to add an identification or disclaimer. The disclaimer or identification, when paid for by a campaign committee, shall be identified by the words 'paid for by' followed by the name and address of the campaign committee and the appropriate officer of the committee, identified by name and title.

The ordinary identification duty permits a residence or business address. The political-party committee alternative, personal-correspondence exception, discretionary small-item rulemaking permission, and campaign-committee paid-for-by requirement have distinct triggers. Personal correspondence is excepted only when not machine-reproduced for general distribution. The secretary may grant exemptions by rule; the listed objects are not automatically exempt and exemption is not mandatory. No particular implementing rule is established in this record. No established fact places these leaflets within a committee provision or exemption; that source limit is not a finding that no exemption rule existed. Subsection (B) is not adjudicated.

Chief Justice Alex-Lamar Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer participated at argument and decision.

#### Judgment

The Supreme Court of Ohio's judgment is reversed, 8–1. The enforcement order and $100 fine imposed for these leaflets must be set aside.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Remedy or remand |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Ohio judgment sustaining identification requirement and fine for Margaret McIntyre's leaflets | Reverse, 8–1 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia | Further proceedings must set aside the enforcement order and $100 fine; valid disclosure rules not inconsistent with the holding remain enforceable. |

The judgment is as applied to this individual's issue leaflets. It awards no damages and does not invalidate every subsection, candidate-finance report, committee disclaimer, organizational disclosure, or rule directed at fraudulent impersonation.

#### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Reverse the Ohio judgment | Exacting scrutiny, insufficient information and fraud justifications in this application, reserved disclosure settings, and setting aside the sanction. |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Thomas | No other Justice | Reverse the Ohio judgment | Anonymous political-pamphlet protection under Talley and its recognized historical tradition; does not join the Court's modern balancing and content-based-scrutiny method. |
| Dissent | Scalia | No other Justice | Would affirm this application | Election-specific attribution as a limited accountability safeguard, preserving distinct protection where threats or reprisals are established. |

#### Controlling Propositions and Authority

##### Compelled identification on these political leaflets requires exacting scrutiny

**Controlling proposition:** A content-defined requirement that an individual identify herself on ordinary political issue leaflets burdens core political expression, including the choice to speak anonymously. The State must satisfy exacting scrutiny by showing narrow tailoring to an overriding state interest; viewpoint neutrality or the election setting alone does not lower that standard.

**Authority:** Justice Stevens's opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer: seven direct rationale votes. Justice Thomas concurs only in the judgment on his distinct anonymous-political-pamphlet ground; Justice Scalia dissents.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the author's choice to omit her identity is protected as part of political expression. Talley protects anonymous handbills and recognizes the place of anonymous publication in public discussion. Ohio's requirement selects publications by their electoral subject and conditions their circulation on identification. It is therefore a content-based burden on the individual's communication, not simply a neutral rule for administering a ballot. Burson preserves demanding scrutiny for campaign-specific restrictions even though its different polling-place judgment had no shared validity rationale. Ohio invokes the modest size of the identification obligation and its election-related purpose. Neither answers whether the State may impose the burden on this ordinary issue leaflet. The Court applies exacting scrutiny requiring narrow tailoring to an overriding interest. It does not decide that anonymity is always benign or require proof that this speaker faced retaliation. Candidate finance reports, organizational attribution, coordinated spending, and fraudulent impersonation present distinct questions left outside this holding.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Talley v. California, 362 U.S. 60: applies protection of anonymous handbills to this individual political leaflet; the Court does not establish immunity for fraudulent communications.
- Burson v. Freeman, May 26, 1992: applies the independently majority-supported scrutiny distinction; its fractured polling-place validity judgment does not settle this leaflet requirement.
- Anderson v. Celebrezze, 460 U.S. 780: distinguishes the election-administration setting; it does not reduce scrutiny for every election-related speech restriction.

##### The asserted voter-information interest does not justify this identification demand

**Controlling proposition:** A general interest in helping voters evaluate a source does not justify requiring this ordinary individual to place her name and residence or business address on her independently produced, truthful political leaflet. The Court leaves candidate finance, campaign committees, organizational spending, and other materially distinct disclosure settings for separate analysis.

**Authority:** Justice Stevens's opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer: seven direct rationale votes. Justice Thomas concurs only in the judgment on his distinct anonymous-political-pamphlet ground; Justice Scalia dissents.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that voters' interest in source information does not justify this direct condition on an ordinary citizen's political pamphlet. Talley protects the decision to publish without personal attribution; an author need not establish that identity has no possible informational value. Ohio relies on Buckley's campaign-finance disclosure and Bellotti's discussion of identifying corporate advertising. Those sources concern different mechanisms and interests. A report identifying financial participation in a campaign is not the same requirement as putting a private individual's name and address on every issue leaflet she distributes. Corporate spending likewise supplies no automatic rule for this record, which establishes neither a campaign organization nor coordinated financing. The Court does not hold that source information is useless or that all disclosure is forbidden. It holds that the general informational rationale is inadequate for the burden imposed here. Ohio's committee alternatives and personal-correspondence exception do not transform Margaret McIntyre's independently produced leaflets into those different statutory categories or justify this sanction.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Talley v. California, 362 U.S. 60: applies protection for the ordinary author's choice of anonymous publication.
- Buckley v. Valeo, 424 U.S. 1: distinguishes campaign-finance reporting and its specific interests; those disclosure holdings remain undisturbed.
- First National Bank of Boston v. Bellotti, 435 U.S. 765: distinguishes corporate-advertising source information; its observation does not resolve this private individual's leaflet.

##### Fraud prevention does not justify this application to truthful independent advocacy

**Controlling proposition:** Although preventing electoral fraud is a weighty interest, Ohio may not impose this categorical identification requirement on truthful, independently produced individual issue leaflets without the necessary narrow fit to that interest. The enforcement order and $100 fine must be set aside; properly tailored fraud, impersonation, and materially different disclosure rules remain available.

**Authority:** Justice Stevens's opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer: seven direct rationale votes. Justice Thomas concurs only in the judgment on his distinct anonymous-political-pamphlet ground; Justice Scalia dissents.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that Ohio's fraud-prevention interest does not supply the missing fit. Talley protects anonymous expression without preventing laws directed at false or fraudulent communications. Ohio's identification requirement reaches ordinary truthful advocacy regardless of any finding of deception, false attribution, or corrupt financing. The record contains no adjudicated fraud in McIntyre's leaflet. The State argues that identification helps investigators find wrongdoers and deters unlawful claims before they affect an election. That practical benefit does not explain why every individual issue pamphlet must carry a name and address when existing laws address fraud and false attribution more directly. The personal-correspondence exception and permission to exempt impractical small items do not cure this application to printed leaflets; no implementing exemption rule resolves the fine presented. The Court neither assumes that anonymity guarantees honest speech nor invalidates all disclosure provisions. It sets aside this sanction, preserves properly tailored enforcement against actual abuse, and leaves organizational and financial-reporting requirements outside the judgment.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Talley v. California, 362 U.S. 60: applies anonymous-publication protection while distinguishing measures directed at fraud.
- Buckley v. Valeo, 424 U.S. 1: leaves candidate-finance disclosure and corruption-related interests intact rather than treating them as dispositive of this ordinary leaflet.
- First National Bank of Boston v. Bellotti, 435 U.S. 765: leaves materially different corporate source-disclosure questions outside the individual sanction decided here.

#### Precedent Treatment and Current-Law Effect

Talley's anonymous-handbill protection applies to this individual's political issue leaflet. Burson's majority-supported scrutiny distinction remains separate from its fractured validity judgment concerning polling-place environs. Anderson's election-administration framework does not decide this direct speech burden. Buckley's campaign-finance disclosure holdings and Bellotti's corporate-advertising discussion concern materially different interests and mechanisms and remain undisturbed. No precedent is overruled.

Effective April 19, 1995, the State may not enforce this name-and-residence-or-business-address requirement against the truthful, independently produced individual political leaflets at issue. Seven Justices directly adopt exacting scrutiny and the inadequate information and fraud-prevention justifications; Thomas supplies a separate eighth judgment vote. No Marks synthesis is needed. The decision leaves candidate and committee disclosures, coordinated expenditures, large financial contributions, organizational spending, fraudulent impersonation, and appropriately tailored fraud regulation outside the adjudicated application. It does not create an automatic personal-correspondence or small-item exemption, decide an unproduced exemption rule, or adjudicate subsection (B).

#### Separate Positions Relevant to the Decision

Justice Thomas concurs in the judgment. Talley's protection and the historical expressive tradition it recognizes support an individual's anonymous political pamphlet. The asserted justification cannot sustain a penalty imposed solely for missing identification on this ordinary leaflet. He does not adopt the Court's modern value-balancing and content-based-scrutiny methodology as his own ground. His agreement in this judgment therefore adds no vote to the Court's complete scrutiny explanation, and his writing does not invalidate every financing or organizational disclosure rule.

Justice Scalia dissents. Election-specific attribution is a limited accountability requirement that helps voters identify a communication's source and assists enforcement against electoral wrongdoing. Buckley's disclosure principles and Bellotti's source-information discussion support that safeguard in his view. Talley's general prohibition covering all anonymous handbills is distinguishable from Ohio's election category. He recognizes the expressive burden but regards the State's justification as sufficient here; he would sustain the fine. He preserves appropriate protection where a speaker establishes threats or reprisals, without finding that McIntyre experienced either. He does not endorse a general power to forbid anonymous discussion outside the election setting.

#### Source Notes

The complete official Ohio opinion, McIntyre v. Ohio Elections Commission, 67 Ohio St.3d 391 (1993), supplies Margaret's conduct, the administrative fine, lower-court review, and Wright's dissent. The [official United States Reports, 514 U.S. 334](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep514/usrep514334/usrep514334.pdf), caption and page 340, supplies executor capacity; pages 338–339 note 3 reproduce the quoted 1988 subsection (A), including its qualifications. These locations support objective party and statutory facts. No executor first name, death date, substitution date, implementing exemption rule, personal threat, fraud finding, or founding-era source detail is inferred. The complete subsection (A) quotation is statutory text, not a quotation from this Court's reasoning; subsection (B) is not represented as supplied.

#### Mandate, Remedy, and Stage

The Supreme Court of Ohio's judgment is reversed. Further proceedings must set aside the enforcement order and $100 fine against Margaret McIntyre, whose estate's executor maintains the challenge. Ohio may enforce valid disclosure rules consistent with the holding. The Court orders no damages, replacement election procedure, blanket statutory repeal, or retained Supreme Court proceeding.

**End of entry: McIntyre, Executor of Estate of McIntyre, Deceased v. Ohio Elections Commission, merits decision, April 19, 1995.**

---

## Earlier published position reading copy: Colorado Republican Federal Campaign Committee v. Federal Election Commission, No. 95-489

Source: [OT_1995CHUNK10.md](../../OT1995/output/OT_1995CHUNK10.md). Copied verbatim; its published scope controls.

### Colorado Republican Federal Campaign Committee v. Federal Election Commission, No. 95-489

Merits decision, June 26, 1996

#### Chronology and Posture

Argued April 15, 1996; decided June 26, 1996. On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Tenth Circuit, 59 F.3d 1015, under 28 U.S.C. §1254(1).

The question is whether the First Amendment permits applying the federal party-expenditure limit to an expenditure made independently of a candidate. The Party also seeks broader declaratory relief concerning the Senate-election spending ceiling. That counterclaim raises a separate question about the decision's permissible reach.

In April 1986, before either party nominated its Senate candidate, the state committee paid $15,000 for a radio advertisement criticizing Democratic candidate Timothy Wirth's record. The committee had assigned its entire $103,248 adjusted allowance for the Senate contest to the National Republican Senatorial Committee. The District Court entered summary judgment for the Party and dismissed its constitutional counterclaim as moot. The Tenth Circuit reversed and directed judgment for the FEC and determination of an appropriate civil penalty. No penalty amount had been fixed.

All nine Justices participated at argument and decision.

#### Judgment

**Vacated and remanded, 6–3.**

**Supporting:** O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Breyer.

**Opposing:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens and Ginsburg.

The direction to enter judgment for the FEC and assess a penalty for this independent advertisement cannot stand. No damages, refund, fixed penalty or new factual trial is ordered. The six supporting Justices agree on that relief but divide over the broader constitutional counterclaim. The Court grants no facial declaration concerning coordinated party expression and does not affirm the counterclaim's mootness dismissal as a substantive rejection of that claim. Its further treatment remains for the proceedings below, subject to the controlling protection of this advertisement. The dissent would affirm enforcement and leave the amount of a penalty to the lower court.

#### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Principal opinion, Part I | O'Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Opinion of the Court on the limited independent-advertisement and statutory-predicate propositions | First Amendment protection of this genuinely independent advertisement; party status cannot itself supply the candidate-connection predicate for the alternative contribution theory |
| Principal opinion, Parts II–III | O'Connor | Souter, Breyer | Plurality explanation supporting vacatur and remand | Complete independent-expenditure framework and proposed separate consideration of the remaining constitutional counterclaim |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Kennedy | Scalia, Thomas | Joins Part I and the relief protecting this advertisement; seeks additional constitutional relief | Party-authored Senate-campaign expression remains protected despite cooperation with a candidate; distinct transfers and genuine donor conduits remain outside that proposed rule |
| Dissent | Stevens | Stone-Zsela, Ginsburg | Would affirm enforcement, with penalty assessment below | Institution-specific contribution-channel, corruption and circumvention grounds; no equalization ground or finding of actual coordination |

No majority joins the complete narrower framework or the broader coordinated-expression rule. Part I controls through its six express joins. The Court does not derive an additional controlling rationale by combining the other writings.

#### Controlling Propositions and Authority

##### Independent party expression

**Controlling proposition:** A political party's own campaign advertisement that is genuinely independent of a candidate may not be subjected to the Senate-election expenditure ceiling in 2 U.S.C. §441a(d)(3) merely because its speaker is an official political party. Party identity and a continuing electoral relationship do not, without more, justify applying that ceiling to this independent expression; the adverse enforcement judgment must therefore be vacated.

**Authority:** Part I of O'Connor's opinion, with the six-Justice coalition identified above. This is their expressly shared sufficient ground for protecting the independent advertisement, not a six-Justice adoption of every further explanation or reservation.

**Controlling explanation:** Paying for its own political message directly exercises the Party's speech and its members' association. Buckley distinguishes independent expression from resources furnished to a candidate; NCPAC protects genuinely independent advocacy against expenditure limits. Those principles do not disappear when the independent speaker is a political party. The committee's general purpose of electing candidates does not establish that a candidate directed, requested or helped arrange this advertisement.

The government relies on the continuing party–candidate relationship and the risk that donors will use parties to evade candidate limits. Corruption and realistic circumvention are legitimate concerns. But those concerns do not justify treating this independently arranged message as a candidate contribution solely from the speaker's identity. Day likewise preserves both meaningful collective advocacy and regulation of real contribution conduits; its intermediary holding did not decide the separately excluded party category, so the present conclusion rests on this transaction and the independent-expression authorities.

The advertisement preceded nomination, and the record supplies no actual candidate connection overcoming its independence. The fact that the adjusted party allowance had already been assigned explains why the FEC sought enforcement; it does not cure the constitutional defect. The Court requires neither proof of a completed bribe in every financing case nor immunity for every party payment. Whether coordinated party-authored expression receives the additional protection urged by three Justices is not resolved by a majority.

##### The alternative contribution theory

**Controlling proposition:** For the government's theory that this advertisement is a contribution under §441a(a)(7)(B)(i), party status alone cannot substitute for expenditure made in cooperation, consultation or concert with, or at the request or suggestion of, a candidate, the candidate's authorized political committees or their agents. On the accepted independence premise, that theory cannot preserve the adverse enforcement judgment under §441a(a)(2).

**Authority:** Part I's same six-Justice coalition. The proposition concerns the required connection and this alternative theory; it does not decide the constitutionality of every transaction in which the connection is present.

**Controlling explanation:** Congress stated a candidate-connection predicate. The agency's categorical description of party activity does not make that predicate a factual finding about this advertisement. Nor does the government's assertion that its theory was uncontested establish an actual concession. The Party's claim that the theory was newly raised supplies no independent forfeiture holding.

The separate contribution provisions therefore do not automatically disappear when the expenditure ceiling cannot be applied. They remain subject to their own definitions and qualifications, including the exemption from §441a(a)(1) and (2) for transfers among national, State, district, local and subordinate committees of the same political party. Financing the dissemination, distribution or republication of candidate-prepared materials under §441a(a)(7)(B)(ii) and attributing earmarked or intermediary-directed contributions to their original source under §441a(a)(8) present different predicates. None is established for this advertisement. The decision does not settle every interaction between those provisions and §441a(d)(1)'s notwithstanding clause.

#### Limits and Questions Not Reached

The Court establishes no general exemption for coordinated party spending, direct candidate transfers, candidate cash, earmarked donations, republication, sham independence or demonstrated donor conduits. It also sustains none of those applications by implication. The broader Senate-election counterclaim remains without a majority constitutional resolution; only three Justices would invalidate the ceiling for party-authored expression even when coordinated, and only three join the complete prudential rationale for reserving that issue.

The presidential allowance in §441a(d)(2), other House-election limits, a permanent constitutional dollar floor, a universal vagueness ruling and a general administrative-deference rule are not decided. The Court does not hold that the absence of a particular verbal formula always excludes election-related speech from the statute. No Justice adopts a general rejection of the contribution/expenditure distinction.

#### Precedent Treatment

- **Buckley v. Valeo, 424 U.S. 1:** Its distinction between independent expenditures and candidate contributions supplies the present independent-advertisement rule. Its treatment of the party provision's Fifth Amendment challenge did not decide this First Amendment question. The general distinction remains in force.
- **FEC v. National Conservative Political Action Committee, 470 U.S. 480:** Its protection of genuinely independent political advocacy supports the result; it supplies no categorical immunity for actual contribution channels.
- **Day v. Holahan, May 15, 1995:** Its controlling protection of meaningful intermediary association and preservation of realistic conduit regulation inform the required distinction. Its recipient category excluded political-party units, and its responsive-financing judgment had no unified controlling rationale. Neither is converted into an earlier decision of this party-spending question.
- **FEC v. Massachusetts Citizens for Life, Inc., 479 U.S. 238:** Its protection of political expression and its nonprofit setting remain distinct; the Court does not rest today's judgment on a universal express-advocacy formula or extend the nonprofit exception to every entity.

#### Current Law

Section 441a(d)(3) cannot support enforcement against genuinely independent party campaign expression at the scope decided here. A party-status presumption also cannot alone supply the actual candidate connection required by the government's alternative contribution theory. The holding adds this protection to the existing contribution/expenditure distinction without repealing the separate contribution channels.

There is no controlling rule today either invalidating all coordinated party-expression limits or approving their constitutionality. Neither plurality treatment becomes law through the six votes for protecting this independent advertisement. Ordinary contribution doctrine, the distinct rules for candidate-prepared material and earmarked contributions, and their operative qualifications remain available within their own scopes.

#### Separate Positions Relevant to the Decision

**O'Connor's Parts II–III, joined by Souter and Breyer.** These Justices apply the independent-expenditure framework while preserving a separate inquiry into actual candidate resources and preventive contribution controls. Buckley supplies the functional distinction; MCFL and NCPAC support protection of independent expression; Day preserves both collective association and realistic conduit regulation. They give legitimate predictions about corruption their due but reject replacing the advertisement's actual circumstances with a party label. They do not demand a proved bribe for every preventive rule.

They would address the remaining counterclaim separately because party-authored expression, direct payment of a candidate's bills and undifferentiated transfers may involve different constitutional and statutory questions. The relation between the party allowance and ordinary contribution limits, and any necessary severability issue, should receive proper treatment before a broader declaration. The counterclaim is not jurisdictionally barred, and the advertisement need not be retried merely to recreate a fact of coordination. Their proposed restraint is supported by these three Justices; it is not the full rationale of the six-Justice holding.

**Kennedy's concurrence, joined by Scalia and Thomas.** These Justices accept Part I's protection of the independent advertisement but find that relief insufficient. A party pursues its own political program through the candidates it supports. Its own campaign message does not become another person's speech merely because the party and candidate cooperate. Buckley's valid treatment of ordinary contributions therefore does not decide the distinct restriction on that expression. Kennedy's Austin position and Day's protection of political association support the distinction; Day's preserved contribution rule continues to govern genuine contribution channels.

The broad Senate-election counterclaim and allegation that the ceiling chilled additional spending warrant resolving that party-expression claim now, in their view. They would invalidate §441a(d)(3)'s ceiling on the Party's own Senate-campaign expression, including expression produced in cooperation with its candidate, and remand for residual statutory and remedial consequences. They do not invalidate every §441a(a)(2) recipient limit, decide presidential financing, immunize undifferentiated party transfers or candidate cash, or protect donors using earmarked or sham conduits. Thomas joins this party-specific ground within Buckley's framework; he proposes no general merger of contributions and expenditures.

**Stevens's dissent, joined by Stone-Zsela and Ginsburg.** These Justices accept that the advertisement was independently arranged. They nevertheless regard the official party's continuing selection, support and financing of its candidates as a distinctive contribution channel. Donors who may give more through party channels than directly to a candidate could evade lawful candidate limits if all party election spending were uncapped. The official nominating institution's relationship survives the fact that this particular advertisement preceded nomination. Congress may address that institutional risk without proving an actual bargain or consultation for each advertisement.

They would uphold this election-related, inflation-adjusted allowance because it leaves meaningful collective advocacy while constraining the contribution channel. Ordinary issue discussion outside the statute's relevant candidate-election coverage is not automatically included. Buckley supplies legitimate contribution and anti-circumvention interests, and Day preserves realistic conduit regulation while requiring fit: Day's poorly fitted $100 intermediary ceiling does not itself decide this different party allowance. They do not rely on equalizing influence, reducing campaign costs or disliking expensive speech, and they do not extend their conclusion to every PAC, individual, press organization or nonprofit. The dissent would affirm enforcement under §441a(d), leave the penalty amount for the lower court and reject the requested facial invalidation without adjudicating every hypothetical coordinated transaction or finding an independent §441a(a)(2) violation.

#### Source Notes

The [Tenth Circuit opinion, 59 F.3d 1015](https://static.case.law/f3d/59/html/1015-01.html), supplies the judgment below and its direction to assess a civil penalty; the District Court opinion is reported at 839 F. Supp. 1448. The [No.95-489 petition, briefs and Joint Appendix](https://archive.org/details/micro_IA40385013_0654) supply the advertisement, testimony and competing submissions. The [official 1994 text of §441a](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USCODE-1994-title2/html/USCODE-1994-title2-chap14-subchapI-sec441a.htm) supplies the distinct expenditure and contribution provisions. The agency's categorical coordination description and the parties' assertions about an alternative theory are legal contentions, not findings of actual consultation, concession or forfeiture. No fixed penalty or later remand event is established.

#### Mandate, Remedy, and Stage

The case returns through the Tenth Circuit. Its prior instruction to enter judgment for the FEC and determine a penalty for the independent advertisement is displaced. Further proceedings must respect the six-Justice protection of that advertisement and address the remaining counterclaim and any genuinely remaining statutory or remedial questions without treating either three-Justice constitutional approach as the Court's holding. No particular subsequent filing, evidentiary hearing, payment or disposition of the broader claim is ordered or presumed.

**End of entry: Colorado Republican Federal Campaign Committee v. Federal Election Commission, Merits decision, June 26, 1996.**

---

## Earlier published position reading copy: Lopez v. Monterey County, No. 95-1201

Source: [OT_1996CHUNK1.md](../../OT1996/output/OT_1996CHUNK1.md). Copied verbatim; its published scope controls.

### Lopez v. Monterey County, No. 95-1201

Merits decision, November 6, 1996

#### Chronology and Posture

Argued October 8, 1996. On appeal from the three-judge United States District Court for the Northern District of California. The court authorized March 1996 municipal-court elections under a countywide, at-large system without preclearance of all operative changes and joined California for further litigation about the source of the system. Review concerns whether a covered County must preclear state-mandated changes despite having no policy discretion; the direct voting change, designation and prospective-laches defenses; and the limits on an enforcement court's use of an unprecleared governmental plan and a wholly court-developed temporary remedy. The plaintiffs seek reversal, prospective nonuse and lawful preclearance or interim proceedings.

#### Judgment

**Reversed and remanded, 9–0.**

**Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer.

The order authorizing the March 1996 countywide, at-large election is vacated. The district court must enjoin prospective administration of that system unless and until every operative post-November 1, 1968 voting change necessary to it has received §5 preclearance. It must determine the exact scope of the 1983 submission, identify changes still unprecleared, and require their expeditious submission through the Attorney General or the United States District Court for the District of Columbia. It must consider any necessary interim arrangement only within independent lawful remedial authority and the limits stated below. The Court orders no completed election undone, judicial office vacated, judicial act invalidated or particular replacement plan adopted.

#### Opinions and Joins

Justice Kennedy delivers the opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Its Parts I–IV correspond to the four controlling holdings below. Chief Justice Stone-Zsela files a concurrence, joined by no other Justice, adding his constitutional response to the designation objection and specifying his boundary for a wholly judicial plan.

#### Holdings

**Common authority:** Parts I–IV of Kennedy's unanimous opinion control the four holdings below. The additional grounds in the concurrence do not form part of those holdings.

##### I. State compulsion does not exempt a covered County's administration

**Rule:** A covered political subdivision administers a voting change when its officials give the change operative effect within the subdivision. Section 5 therefore requires Monterey County to obtain preclearance before administering a postbaseline judicial-election change even if noncovered California compels the change and leaves the County no discretion; the duty is the County's and does not require California to preclear its laws in the abstract.

The Court holds that execution of an electoral change is distinct from enactment of it. Section 5 separately reaches a covered jurisdiction that enacts or seeks to administer a change. A condition of local policy discretion would erase the second route whenever a noncovered State required its covered subdivision to operate the new system. The statute contains no such condition. Monterey County conducts these public judicial elections and gives the countywide electorate operative effect; the voters experience the same change regardless of its legislative origin.

Presley supplies the distinction between a change in voting and a transfer of internal governing duties. Morse confirms the importance of the practice's actual electoral operation while preserving the designated substantive-preclearance process. Neither already decided this local-discretion question, and the Court adopts no general state-delegation test. The holding answers the legal question even assuming California law entirely displaced the County ordinances; it does not decide that disputed state-law characterization. Nor does it impose a statewide submission obligation, determine discriminatory purpose or retrogression, or confer general federal power to redesign California's judiciary.

##### II. Electorate changes and the limits of prior submission

**Rule:** Replacing nine district electorates with one countywide electorate for municipal judges is a change affecting voting under §§5 and 14(c)(1). A later related submission does not preclear earlier changes that it failed to identify; the district court must determine the precise reach of the 1983 submission as to the final ordinance while treating the earlier unidentified consolidations as unprecleared.

The Court holds that this consolidation changes who elects each judge, rather than merely the work performed after election. At the November 1, 1968 baseline, voters elected the judges of their separate districts. The six ordinances adopted between 1972 and 1983 ultimately placed the judges before one countywide electorate. Presley preserves coverage for that direct alteration of electoral choice. Allen and Perkins supply the same distinction through their treatment of electoral mechanisms and boundaries.

McCain and Clark require an actual, identifiable submission of the change for which preclearance is claimed; ambiguity is not blanket approval of earlier practices. The record establishes that California's 1983 submission did not identify or describe the earlier ordinances. Whether it reached the final 1983 ordinance must be determined from the submission's actual terms. The County cannot obtain approval of the whole consolidation sequence merely by pointing to that later submission. Coverage and lack of preclearance establish the nonuse obligation, not a finding that the system has discriminatory purpose or retrogressive effect.

##### III. Effective designation and prospective enforcement

**Rule:** Section 4(b)'s administrative coverage determination may not be collaterally retried in this §5 enforcement action; statutory bailout under §4(a) is a distinct route. Delay cannot authorize prospective administration of an unprecleared change, although reliance, completed acts and practical timing may bear on lawfully available retrospective relief or the form of a lawful transition.

The Court holds that the County's published designation remains operative in this case. Congress separately restricted review of the coverage determination and provided a judicial bailout process. The County has not obtained release from coverage through that process. Its objection cannot convert this enforcement suit into a collateral retrial of the administrative findings.

Nor does the age of the ordinances excuse their future use without submission. Section 5 conditions administration on preclearance; the passage of time supplies no substitute. Clark enforces that statutory sequence, and Hampton County confirms that elapsed time and electoral implementation do not themselves remove the review obligation. The practical cost of delay warrants a fitted transition, not continued use of the same uncleared governmental plan as an ordinary electoral system. The Court makes no factual bailout determination, awards no damages, and decides no backward-looking relief concerning a completed election, judicial tenure or completed judicial acts.

##### IV. Enforcement and a genuinely judicial temporary remedy

**Rule:** A §5 enforcement court may determine coverage and submission status and enforce nonuse, but may neither grant substantive preclearance nor authorize an unprecleared County or State electoral plan by calling it court ordered. A necessary, temporary plan wholly formulated by the court may operate without separate §5 preclearance only when independent lawful remedial authority supports it; it must comply with the Constitution and Voting Rights Act, displace state law no further than necessary, and yield to a lawful precleared arrangement.

The Court holds that judicial approval does not change the governmental origin of an electoral plan. Connor distinguishes a wholly judicial remedy; McDaniel prevents a court order from insulating governmental policy choices from submission. Here the district court reinstated substantially the County's at-large system and authorized ordinary six-year terms. It did not formulate a wholly judicial temporary remedy. Clark's reservation for an extreme circumstance supplies no basis on this record: prolonged notice of the problem, abandonment of the County's D.C. preclearance action and ordinary terms do not establish a last-minute necessity. This decision recognizes no broader emergency exception.

Morse and Shaw preserve Congress's separate preclearance channels. The enforcement court cannot decide purpose or effect itself, even in light of the County's stipulation concerning its inability to prove nonretrogression. Administrative difficulty does not enlarge its authority.

The earlier four-division arrangement is a material option for consideration. It retained a single municipal court and received preclearance on March 6, 1995; its terms reportedly end in January 1997. Johnson v. Miller prevents racial predominance in a shared territorial electorate, standing alone, from supplying a constitutional defect. But neither that rule nor preclearance answers the separate objections concerning electoral and jurisdictional linkage, minimum population, divided municipalities and candidate residence, or establishes independent remedial authority. The Court selects no interim plan. Those matters, the exact 1983 submission and any actual officeholder circumstances require lawful determination below.

#### Precedent Treatment

- **Presley v. Etowah County Commission; Mack v. Russell County Commission:** Distinguishes internal allocations of governing authority from the direct electorate change here; the former exclusion remains intact.
- **Morse v. Republican Party of Virginia:** Applies its bounded concern with actual electoral operation and its separate preclearance channels without enlarging its party-convention holding; no general delegation test or new private-action rule is adopted.
- **Allen v. State Board of Elections:** Applies protection of electoral choice to the changed means of electing judges.
- **Perkins v. Matthews:** Applies coverage of changes in electoral boundaries and the electorate, without equating coverage with discrimination.
- **McCain v. Lybrand:** Requires identification of the submitted change and rejects blanket approval of unidentified earlier practices.
- **Clark v. Roemer:** Enforces nonuse pending preclearance and preserves, without applying, its narrow extreme-circumstance reservation; the present ordinary six-year elections do not qualify.
- **NAACP v. Hampton County Election Commission:** Passage of time and implementation do not eliminate the submission requirement; actual circumstances remain relevant to lawful relief.
- **Connor v. Johnson:** Retains the distinction for a wholly judicial remedial plan.
- **McDaniel v. Sanchez:** A court's adoption of governmental policy does not transform it into an exempt judicial plan.
- **Shaw v. Reno, designated-channel holding:** Substantive preclearance remains with the Attorney General or D.D.C.; no enforcement-court substitute is authorized.
- **Johnson v. Miller:** Racial predominance in a shared territorial electorate alone does not establish constitutional injury or trigger strict scrutiny; separate statutory and constitutional objections remain open.

#### Law After the Decision

Section 5 now expressly reaches a covered County's nondiscretionary administration of state-mandated voting changes. The election must not proceed under the unprecleared governmental system merely because the State requires it, delay has occurred or a federal court has ordered it used. Existing direct-voting coverage, specific-submission, designated-channel and genuinely judicial-remedy rules remain. The new construction neither places all California law under §5 nor changes the substantive purpose/effect burdens. The temporary divisions remain an option for lawful consideration, with their independent defects and authority unresolved. No new general rule governing retrospective equitable relief is announced.

#### Separate Writings

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela concurs in the Court's entire opinion and judgment. He also concludes that South Carolina v. Katzenbach, which sustains the Act's coverage and preclearance machinery, and City of Rome, which sustains its operation against covered governmental units, foreclose the facial constitutional objection to the County's operative designation. This is distinct from the Court's statutory bar on collateral retrial of the coverage findings. He further states that the wholly judicial exception does not apply when a court adopts material electoral policy choices proposed, required or previously selected by the County or the State. Necessity and independent remedial authority must support any genuinely court-formulated temporary plan; he selects no plan and decides no purpose or effect question. No other Justice joins these additional formulations.

#### Source Notes

The November 1, 1968 baseline, 1972–1983 consolidation sequence, disputed 1983 submission and order under review are the established record posture. The [appellants' merits brief, No. 95-1201](https://archive.org/details/micro_IA40385013_0689), document 08, identifies the temporary division proceedings (JA123–137), the challenged order (JA165–173, 180) and the March 6, 1995 preclearance letter (JS App53–55). Its account of state-law objections and the district court's reasons remains a source-specific account, not an independent finding that the temporary plan violates any law. This appeal establishes no intervening Supreme Court stay, completed March election, cancellation, holdover or vacancy; the decision's prospective commands do not depend on any such event.

#### Mandate, Remedy, and Stage

The Supreme Court appeal is completed. The district court must enforce nonuse, determine the actual submission history, require expeditious use of the designated preclearance channels and consider any independently authorized interim arrangement. No deadline, permanent electoral plan, completed-election consequence or specific judicial tenure outcome is fixed here.

**End of entry: Lopez v. Monterey County, Merits decision, November 6, 1996.**

---

## Effective current-term public law: NAACP, Los Angeles Branch v. Jones ? 1998-11-05

Source: [bounded public reading copy](naacp_los_angeles_branch_v_jones_merits_1998-11-05.md). Copied verbatim; effective before all group A events.

# Current-term public reading copy

## Event

NAACP, Los Angeles Branch v. Jones, No. 97-1840. Decided November 5, 1998. Reasoned threshold decision after full merits briefing, on writ of certiorari to the Ninth Circuit; no argument date is established. The judgment under review is *NAACP, Los Angeles Branch v. Jones*, 131 F.3d 1317 (9th Cir. 1997), decided December 16, 1997, No. 96-56455, affirming dismissal of the constitutional challenge.

The presented questions concern whether privately financed judicial elections deny less wealthy voters and candidates equal protection under the wealth-primary theory and whether reimbursement for an optional candidate statement in a mailed sample ballot violates the First or Fourteenth Amendment. The antecedent issue is whether the court below could assume disputed Article III standing before deciding those merits. The plaintiffs seek prospective declaratory and injunctive relief; they expressly exclude an indigent-candidate claim, and the state-law claims were not preserved.

## Participation

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer participate at submission and decision. No recusal or nonparticipation is established.

## Public Action

The Court vacates the Ninth Circuit's judgment and remands for an actual determination of standing before any merits ruling, unanimously.

## Judgment & Remedy

**Disposition:** Vacated and remanded, 9-0. **Supporting:** Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. No Justice opposes.

This single threshold disposition applies to both the wealth-primary/public-financing claim and the optional candidate-statement charge. All nine agree that neither merits component is reached; there is no affirmance or rejection of either constitutional claim. The mandate requires an actual standing determination under *Lujan* and *Steel Co.* on the pleadings and appropriate record. It establishes neither standing nor the absence of standing for every plaintiff and directs no merits result. No injunction, campaign funding, free publication or damages award issues.

## Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer | Supports the entire vacatur and remand | Entire opinion: jurisdictional rule, stage-appropriate inquiry, limits and nonreach of both merits components |

No partial join, judgment-only concurrence or separate writing issues.

## Holdings

### Actual Article III standing before contested constitutional merits

**Holding and operative rule:** A federal court may not assume disputed Article III standing to decide these contested constitutional claims about judicial-election financing and optional candidate statements. It must determine, with support appropriate to the litigation stage, whether the plaintiffs have a concrete and particularized, actual or imminent injury fairly traceable to the challenged conduct and likely to be redressed by the prospective relief they seek; the Ninth Circuit's merits judgment is vacated so that determination can be made on the actual pleadings and appropriate record.

**Authority:** Scalia's Opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. All nine participating Justices adopt this rule, its explanation and the remand limits below; it controls directly without any fractured-opinion inquiry.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the disputed jurisdictional question must be answered before these constitutional merits. *Citizens for a Better Environment v. Steel Co.* forbids deciding contested merits on assumed Article III power. *Lujan v. Defenders of Wildlife* supplies personal injury, fair traceability and likely redress, with support suited to the litigation stage; *Bennett v. Spear* applies those requirements at pleading without demanding proof appropriate only at a later stage. The District Court found no injury in fact as to the county defendants, but the Ninth Circuit expressly assumed standing to reject the constitutional theories. An unsuccessful claim to free publication or public financing does not itself establish an absence of factual injury, just as a claimed constitutional entitlement does not itself establish standing.

The apparent weakness of a subsidy claim supplies no jurisdictional shortcut. *Libertarian Party of Florida v. Smith*, which examined the actual burden of a different election subsidy, does not settle these plaintiffs' standing or this optional-statement charge. *Norton v. Mathews* and *Secretary of the Navy v. Avrech* retain the confined settings, preserved in *Steel Co.*, in which other controlling decisions had already disposed of the merits; no such controlling resolution of this distinct dispute is established. The Court prescribes no exhaustive order among all nonmerits questions.

The available account identifies a former candidate and prospective relief but does not establish every material allegation in the complaint. The Court therefore neither invents a future candidacy nor treats an allegation omitted from that account as absent from the complaint. This uncertainty supports remand for an actual determination, not a finding that standing exists or a direction to dismiss every plaintiff. Both constitutional merits questions remain open if jurisdiction is established.

**Remand limits and questions not reached:** The lower courts must determine standing on the actual pleadings and appropriate record before any merits ruling. The Court orders no particular evidentiary hearing, standing discovery, document production or amendment entitlement. It neither approves the optional charge nor requires campaign financing or free publication. No indigent-candidate claim is decided, and the unpreserved state-law claims are not revived. No finding of racial intent or viewpoint discrimination, new future-candidacy allegation, ballot-access ruling or rule for a compulsory filing fee is supplied. Merits arguments about the wealth-primary theory and optional printing costs are not alternative holdings.

## Precedent Treatment

- *Citizens for a Better Environment v. Steel Co.*, June 2, 1997: applied to prohibit assumed disputed Article III standing as a basis for these contested constitutional merits; its nonexhaustive treatment of threshold sequencing remains intact. Its direction to dismiss on the different completed-injury record is not transferred here.
- *Lujan v. Defenders of Wildlife*, 504 U.S. 555 (1992): applied for personal injury, traceability, redressability and support appropriate to the litigation stage; its summary-judgment evidentiary demand is not imposed on these pleadings, and its noncontrolling absent-agency application is not enlarged.
- *Bennett v. Spear*, March 19, 1997: applied for stage-appropriate pleading of causal and remedial links; its supported ongoing injury does not establish injury in this different complaint.
- *Norton v. Mathews*, 427 U.S. 524 (1976): preserved at its already-controlled-merits setting; it supplies no permission to assume standing for this unresolved constitutional dispute.
- *Secretary of the Navy v. Avrech*, 418 U.S. 676 (1974): likewise preserved at its distinct already-controlled-merits setting; the Court does not extend that exception to perceived merits simplicity.
- *Libertarian Party of Florida v. Smith*, September 21, 1998: distinguished because its actual-burden review of a different subsidy resolves neither this standing question nor the optional-statement merits. Its election-law limits remain unchanged.

## Law After Decision

Effective November 5, 1998, the existing Article III elements, stage-appropriate support requirements and prohibition on assumed jurisdiction govern this remand without alteration. This application creates no election-finance holding, public-subsidy entitlement, categorical fee immunity or new standing-proof requirement. The Ninth Circuit's vacated merits judgment supplies no resolution of the parties' constitutional claims; those claims may be addressed only if jurisdiction is established. The Court establishes no mandatory sequence for all nonmerits issues and changes none of the preserved exceptions for merits already disposed of by other controlling decisions.

## Separate Writings

None.

## Procedure After Action

The Supreme Court's review is complete. The judgment of the Ninth Circuit is vacated, and the case returns to that court for an actual standing determination on the pleadings and appropriate record, with further lower-court proceedings as ordinary law permits. If jurisdiction is established, the constitutional merits remain open; this mandate directs no merits outcome. No hearing, amendment, new production requirement, filing deadline or future Supreme Court return is ordered.

## Source Notes

The [published Ninth Circuit opinion](https://caselaw.findlaw.com/court/us-9th-circuit/1257053.html), including footnotes 3-4, supplies the District Court's threshold dismissals and the appellate assumption of standing. It identifies former candidacy and prospective relief without establishing the complaint's complete continuing- or impending-injury allegations. That limitation supports the bounded remand; it proves neither standing nor a missing allegation. The optional statement's cost-reimbursement mechanism is background, not a statutory holding. No precise argument or submission date is established, and the questions are stated in substance rather than quoted from a grant order.

## Published Kentucky Right to Life separate writings

Source: [OT1997 chunk 13](../../OT1997/output/OT_1997CHUNK13.md). Exact section copy.

#### Separate Writings

**Stone-Zsela, concurring in part and dissenting in part; Stevens and Ginsburg join the annual-aggregate dissent only.** The three would affirm §121.150(10)'s annual-aggregate judgment on the preserved facial record. Buckley and California Medical permit preventive regulation of actual candidate-funding intermediaries, and KRLPAC makes candidate transfers. Day requires meaningful pooling and genuine fit, but its established $100 burden does not compel remand whenever a higher limit's practical effects have not been quantified. The valid direct candidate limit and anti-pass-through function suffice here, without declaring intermediary association less valuable or requiring proof of completed corruption. The dissent recognizes that the combined cap substantially burdens multi-organization association. It reserves developed proof of prohibitory cumulative operation, improperly covered independent advocacy and changed conditions, including inflation; its position creates no perpetual $1,500 safe harbor. Stone-Zsela alone further explains that the Fund-participant percentage connects the source ceiling to the slate's own qualifying receipts more closely than a flat maximum. It may bind at lower receipts; its facial justification rests on source-specific dependency and this record, not on a 151st donor. A small-receipt slate's later concrete fundraising disability remains open. That additional comparison is not part of the Court's holding.

**Kennedy, dissenting in part, joined by Scalia and Thomas.** These Justices would vacate and remand both gubernatorial branches for distinct fit analysis. They accept the difference between candidate cash and independent expression and the legitimacy of preventing candidate dependency. But a classwide receipt ceiling excludes later willing donors because others have already given, even though each donor obeys the base limit. Day and the contribution principles preserved in Colorado Republican require the added burden to have its own fitted justification. General hostility toward organized interests and an illustration assuming maximum fundraising and maximum gifts do not supply it, particularly for a percentage that binds earlier. They would remand without facially invalidating either branch, immunizing candidate contributions, or deciding the full constitutionality of voluntary public financing.

**Thomas, dissenting in part.** Thomas would vacate and remand the surviving identification judgment to address the statute's coverage of an individual's independent candidate handbill. Talley and the anonymous-pamphleteering tradition underlying his McIntyre position do not end when the pamphlet discusses a candidate instead of a ballot issue. Identifying organized campaign financing may justify distinct reports, but it does not automatically justify placing an individual's name and address on every protected handbill. He does not find that Zoeller actually distributed one, exempt every organization or medium, select an unverified statutory exception, or order a blanket injunction. His objection seeks proper treatment of the protected-pamphlet breadth, rather than facial invalidation of every identification duty.
